========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, hey... so, I guess I'm here because... man, I don't even really know where to start. Everything's just kind of a mess right now. See, I had to move into this new room a little while back—just a cheap place, you know, 'cause rent is crazy and I just needed a spot—but the place is completely busted up. I mean, the landlord swore it was good to go, but nobody told me the ceiling was practically caving in and the radiator is an absolute joke. 

So every single night, instead of getting some actual sleep, I'm up at like two or three in the morning with a bucket and some caulk trying to fix these stupid leaks, or I'm freezing half to death trying to bang on the pipes to get the heat to kick on. And it's just... it's bleeding into everything else, you know? Like, I'm dragging myself into work—I do construction, mostly heavy labor stuff, hauling and whatever—and I'm rolling in late constantly. The foreman is already on my case about it. 

And because I'm so dead tired and just... wound up from dealing with that stupid room all night, I'm snapping at the other guys on the crew over nothing. Just totally losing my temper if someone even looks at me wrong. Plus, I'm making really dumb mistakes, messing up stuff I normally handle in my sleep. Just dropping things, measuring wrong, you name it. It's like ever since I hauled my stuff into that dump, my whole life is just spinning out, and I can't seem to get my head straight enough to just do my job.

No, not really. I mean, it's not like I don't *want* to do stuff. Like, I still love watching the games on Sunday, you know? Usually, I'd grab a six-pack, maybe head over to my buddy Dave's place, or just kick back and watch football. I still *want* to do that. It's not like I don't care about it or anything. It's just... man, how am I supposed to actually enjoy a game when I'm sitting there dead on my feet, thinking about the fact that I gotta go back to that freezing room and probably patch another leak? 

Or like, last weekend, I was supposed to help my cousin swap out the alternator on his Chevy—which is something I usually really get a kick out of, you know? I like messing around with engines, it's a good time, totally different from just hauling heavy junk around the site all day. And I totally still wanted to do it! I was looking forward to it all week. I just couldn't, because I spent my whole Saturday morning arguing with the guy at the hardware store about the right kind of sealant for this window frame that's completely rotting out on me. 

So, yeah, I still like all my regular stuff. I still get a laugh out of the guys at work when I'm not biting their heads off. I just... I don't have the time or the energy for any of it right now because every spare second is just sucked up by dealing with this nightmare apartment. But the interest is definitely still there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, man, it's... it's like it's glued to the inside of my skull. It's constantly there. Like, even when I'm at the site, right? I'll be hauling a stack of drywall or mixing mud, and normally, I'd just be focused on getting the job done, maybe shooting the breeze with the guys. But now? I'm carrying this heavy stuff, and my brain is just going a mile a minute about that room. I'll be thinking, "Did I put the bucket in the right spot under that one pipe? What if the drip moved? What if it's soaking into the floorboards right now and the guy below me is gonna start screaming about water damage?" 

And it's not just the leaks. It's the cold, too. I'll be sweating on the site because I'm working hard, but I'm dreading the moment I have to clock out because I know I'm going back to a place that feels like a meat locker. I catch myself zoning out while the foreman is giving directions, just trying to mentally calculate if I have enough cash left this week to buy a decent space heater, but then I remember the wiring in that place is so shot that if I plug a heater in, I'll probably blow a fuse for the whole floor. 

Even when I try to relax, like if I actually make it to Dave's place for a bit, I can't even focus on the game. He'll be talking about a play, and I'm just sitting there staring at my phone, wondering if I should text the landlord again, even though I know the guy is just gonna ignore me or tell me he'll "get to it next week" like he always does. It's just... it's exhausting, you know? It's like I'm never actually away from that stupid room, even when I'm miles away from it. It's just eating up all my headspace, every single day.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Man, it's... it's mostly just this huge, overwhelming frustration, you know? Like, I wouldn't say I'm sad, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around crying about it or feeling sorry for myself. It's just... I'm so incredibly ticked off all the time. I mean, think about it—I bust my hump all day at the site, hauling rock, pouring concrete, dealing with all the dust and the noise, and for what? Just to hand over my hard-earned cash to some slumlord who can't even be bothered to make sure the roof over my head actually keeps the water out. 

It makes my blood boil just talking about it. Like, I get so angry I can feel my chest getting tight, and my jaw just clenches up. I'll be lying there at 3 AM, freezing, listening to that *drip, drip, drip* into the bucket, and I just want to put my fist through the wall. But I can't even do that, because then I'd lose my deposit, assuming I even get it back in the first place, which I probably won't, knowing this guy. 

And it's frustrating because I feel so stuck. I don't have the money to just up and move again. You know how much it costs to get a new place? First, last, security deposit—it's thousands of dollars that I just don't have, especially since I'm missing hours at work now because I'm oversleeping from being up all night. So I'm trapped in this freezing, wet box, and there's literally nothing I can do about it except keep patching leaks and arguing with a guy who doesn't care. So yeah, sad isn't the word. It's just this constant, grinding anger and frustration that I can't shake.

It's like ever since I hauled my stuff into that dump, my whole life is just spinning out, and I can't seem to get my head straight enough to just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest or not getting pleasure from things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like I don't *want* to do stuff. Like, I still love watching the games on Sunday, you know? Usually, I'd grab a six-pack, maybe head over to my buddy Dave's place, or just kick back and watch football. I still *want* to do that. It's not like I don't care about it or anything. It's just... man, how am I supposed to actually enjoy a game when I'm sitting there dead on my feet, thinking about the fact that I gotta go back to that freezing room and probably patch another leak? \n\nOr like, last weekend, I was supposed to help my cousin swap out the alternator on his Chevy—which is something I usually really get a kick out of, you know? I like messing around with engines, it's a good time, totally different from just hauling heavy junk around the site all day. And I totally still wanted to do it! I was looking forward to it all week. I just couldn't, because I spent my whole Saturday morning arguing with the guy at the hardware store about the right kind of sealant for this window frame that's completely rotting out on me. \n\nSo, yeah, I still like all my regular stuff. I still get a laugh out of the guys at work when I'm not biting their heads off. I just... I don't have the time or the energy for any of it right now because every spare second is just sucked up by dealing with this nightmare apartment. But the interest is definitely still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about your housing situation even when you're not directly dealing with it, like when you're at work or trying to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, man, it's... it's like it's glued to the inside of my skull. It's constantly there. Like, even when I'm at the site, right? I'll be hauling a stack of drywall or mixing mud, and normally, I'd just be focused on getting the job done, maybe shooting the breeze with the guys. But now? I'm carrying this heavy stuff, and my brain is just going a mile a minute about that room. I'll be thinking, \"Did I put the bucket in the right spot under that one pipe? What if the drip moved? What if it's soaking into the floorboards right now and the guy below me is gonna start screaming about water damage?\" \n\nAnd it's not just the leaks. It's the cold, too. I'll be sweating on the site because I'm working hard, but I'm dreading the moment I have to clock out because I know I'm going back to a place that feels like a meat locker. I catch myself zoning out while the foreman is giving directions, just trying to mentally calculate if I have enough cash left this week to buy a decent space heater, but then I remember the wiring in that place is so shot that if I plug a heater in, I'll probably blow a fuse for the whole floor. \n\nEven when I try to relax, like if I actually make it to Dave's place for a bit, I can't even focus on the game. He'll be talking about a play, and I'm just sitting there staring at my phone, wondering if I should text the landlord again, even though I know the guy is just gonna ignore me or tell me he'll \"get to it next week\" like he always does. It's just... it's exhausting, you know? It's like I'm never actually away from that stupid room, even when I'm miles away from it. It's just eating up all my headspace, every single day.\n\nDoctor: How do you usually feel when you think about your housing situation—do you feel mostly frustrated, sad, or some combination of both?\n\nPatient: Man, it's... it's mostly just this huge, overwhelming frustration, you know? Like, I wouldn't say I'm sad, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around crying about it or feeling sorry for myself. It's just... I'm so incredibly ticked off all the time. I mean, think about it—I bust my hump all day at the site, hauling rock, pouring concrete, dealing with all the dust and the noise, and for what? Just to hand over my hard-earned cash to some slumlord who can't even be bothered to make sure the roof over my head actually keeps the water out. \n\nIt makes my blood boil just talking about it. Like, I get so angry I can feel my chest getting tight, and my jaw just clenches up. I'll be lying there at 3 AM, freezing, listening to that *drip, drip, drip* into the bucket, and I just want to put my fist through the wall. But I can't even do that, because then I'd lose my deposit, assuming I even get it back in the first place, which I probably won't, knowing this guy. \n\nAnd it's frustrating because I feel so stuck. I don't have the money to just up and move again. You know how much it costs to get a new place? First, last, security deposit—it's thousands of dollars that I just don't have, especially since I'm missing hours at work now because I'm oversleeping from being up all night. So I'm trapped in this freezing, wet box, and there's literally nothing I can do about it except keep patching leaks and arguing with a guy who doesn't care. So yeah, sad isn't the word. It's just this constant, grinding anger and frustration that I can't shake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let's see... it's been going on for about a month now. Yeah, right around a month. Because, see, I moved into this place in Bridgeport—just a single room, really, not even a real apartment, but it was all I could swing at the time—I moved in right at the beginning of the month. I thought, you know, "Okay, it's cheap, it's not great, but it's a roof." But man, it wasn't even a week before the whole thing just started falling apart on me. 

I remember the exact day it started getting really bad, actually. It was that first big rainstorm we had a few weeks back, remember that? It was a Tuesday, because I had just gotten paid on Monday, and I was thinking about maybe grabbing a pizza to celebrate getting settled in. But then the rain hit, and I'm sitting there on my mattress, and suddenly I feel this cold drop hit the back of my neck. I look up, and there's a water stain on the ceiling that's just spreading like crazy. Before I knew it, it wasn't just a drip, it was practically raining inside my room. 

So yeah, ever since then, it's just been this constant battle. It's been about four weeks of me trying to patch things up, trying to get the landlord on the phone, trying to stay warm... and it's just been building and building. Every single day for the last month, it's been the same routine. Wake up freezing, check the buckets, go to work exhausted, come home, check the buckets again, try to get a hold of the landlord, get ignored, go to sleep freezing. It's been a solid month of this nightmare, and I don't see an end to it anytime soon, you know? It feels like it's been a year, honestly, but yeah, on the calendar, it's been about a month since shortly after I moved into that room in Bridgeport.

But I can't even do that, because then I'd lose my deposit, assuming I even get it back in the first place, which I probably won't, knowing this guy. \n\nAnd it's frustrating because I feel so stuck. I don't have the money to just up and move again. You know how much it costs to get a new place? First, last, security deposit—it's thousands of dollars that I just don't have, especially since I'm missing hours at work now because I'm oversleeping from being up all night. So I'm trapped in this freezing, wet box, and there's literally nothing I can do about it except keep patching leaks and arguing with a guy who doesn't care. So yeah, sad isn't the word. It's just this constant, grinding anger and frustration that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your housing situation and dealing with these problems?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been going on for about a month now. Yeah, right around a month. Because, see, I moved into this place in Bridgeport—just a single room, really, not even a real apartment, but it was all I could swing at the time—I moved in right at the beginning of the month. I thought, you know, \"Okay, it's cheap, it's not great, but it's a roof.\" But man, it wasn't even a week before the whole thing just started falling apart on me. \n\nI remember the exact day it started getting really bad, actually. It was that first big rainstorm we had a few weeks back, remember that? It was a Tuesday, because I had just gotten paid on Monday, and I was thinking about maybe grabbing a pizza to celebrate getting settled in. But then the rain hit, and I'm sitting there on my mattress, and suddenly I feel this cold drop hit the back of my neck. I look up, and there's a water stain on the ceiling that's just spreading like crazy. Before I knew it, it wasn't just a drip, it was practically raining inside my room. \n\nSo yeah, ever since then, it's just been this constant battle. It's been about four weeks of me trying to patch things up, trying to get the landlord on the phone, trying to stay warm... and it's just been building and building. Every single day for the last month, it's been the same routine. Wake up freezing, check the buckets, go to work exhausted, come home, check the buckets again, try to get a hold of the landlord, get ignored, go to sleep freezing. It's been a solid month of this nightmare, and I don't see an end to it anytime soon, you know? It feels like it's been a year, honestly, but yeah, on the calendar, it's been about a month since shortly after I moved into that room in Bridgeport.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Nah, man, before this? I was... I was completely fine. Like, seriously, I was doing pretty normal before I moved into that room. I mean, sure, work is always tough, hauling all that rock takes it out of you, but I wasn't feeling down or dragging my feet like this. I'd wake up, grab my coffee from the bodega, head to the site, put in my hours, and I actually had energy, you know? I'd hang out with Dave, maybe shoot some pool on the weekends, grab a couple beers, just doing my normal routine. I had motivation. I was actually looking forward to getting my own spot, even if it was just a single room in Bridgeport. 

But then... man, it was like a switch flipped, but only because of this place. All this... this exhaustion, this anger, this feeling like I don't even want to go to work because I'm so drained... it all started within the first couple weeks of finding the water damage and realizing the heat was completely busted. 

Like I was saying about that first storm, right? That first week or so, before the rain, I was okay. I was setting up my little TV on a crate, putting my work boots by the door, thinking I was getting settled. But then the water started coming in, and the temperature dropped, and suddenly I'm spending all my free time emptying buckets and shivering under a sleeping bag. That's exactly when it hit me. That's when I started losing my drive. Because how are you supposed to stay motivated when you're fighting a losing battle against a ceiling that won't stop leaking? It's a hundred percent the room, Doc. If I wasn't dealing with this landlord and this freezing, wet box, I'd be my regular self. I know I would be. I never used to feel like this, not ever. It's just this situation, it's dragging me down so fast, and it all started right after the leaks began.

What if it's soaking into the floorboards right now and the guy below me is gonna start screaming about water damage?\" \n\nAnd it's not just the leaks. It's the cold, too. He'll be talking about a play, and I'm just sitting there staring at my phone, wondering if I should text the landlord again, even though I know the guy is just gonna ignore me or tell me he'll \"get to it next week\" like he always does. It's just... it's exhausting, you know? It's like I'm never actually away from that stupid room, even when I'm miles away from it. Like, I get so angry I can feel my chest getting tight, and my jaw just clenches up. I'll be lying there at 3 AM, freezing, listening to that *drip, drip, drip* into the bucket, and I just want to put my fist through the wall. Like, I get so angry I can feel my chest getting tight, and my jaw just clenches up. I'll be lying there at 3 AM, freezing, listening to that *drip, drip, drip* into the bucket, and I just want to put my fist through the wall. But I can't even do that, because then I'd lose my deposit, assuming I even get it back in the first place, which I probably won't, knowing this guy. \n\nAnd it's frustrating because I feel so stuck. I don't have the money to just up and move again. You know how much it costs to get a new place? First, last, security deposit—it's thousands of dollars that I just don't have, especially since I'm missing hours at work now because I'm oversleeping from being up all night. So I'm trapped in this freezing, wet box, and there's literally nothing I can do about it except keep patching leaks and arguing with a guy who doesn't care. So yeah, sad isn't the word. It's just this constant, grinding anger and frustration that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your housing situation and dealing with these problems?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been going on for about a month now. Yeah, right around a month. Because, see, I moved into this place in Bridgeport—just a single room, really, not even a real apartment, but it was all I could swing at the time—I moved in right at the beginning of the month. I thought, you know, \"Okay, it's cheap, it's not great, but it's a roof.\" But man, it wasn't even a week before the whole thing just started falling apart on me. \n\nI remember the exact day it started getting really bad, actually. It was that first big rainstorm we had a few weeks back, remember that? It was a Tuesday, because I had just gotten paid on Monday, and I was thinking about maybe grabbing a pizza to celebrate getting settled in. But then the rain hit, and I'm sitting there on my mattress, and suddenly I feel this cold drop hit the back of my neck. I look up, and there's a water stain on the ceiling that's just spreading like crazy. Before I knew it, it wasn't just a drip, it was practically raining inside my room. \n\nSo yeah, ever since then, it's just been this constant battle. It's been about four weeks of me trying to patch things up, trying to get the landlord on the phone, trying to stay warm... and it's just been building and building. Every single day for the last month, it's been the same routine. Wake up freezing, check the buckets, go to work exhausted, come home, check the buckets again, try to get a hold of the landlord, get ignored, go to sleep freezing. It's been a solid month of this nightmare, and I don't see an end to it anytime soon, you know? It feels like it's been a year, honestly, but yeah, on the calendar, it's been about a month since shortly after I moved into that room in Bridgeport.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing motivation even before thinking about your housing situation, or do these feelings only come up when you're dealing with the problems in your room?\n\nPatient: Nah, man, before this? I was... I was completely fine. Like, seriously, I was doing pretty normal before I moved into that room. I mean, sure, work is always tough, hauling all that rock takes it out of you, but I wasn't feeling down or dragging my feet like this. I'd wake up, grab my coffee from the bodega, head to the site, put in my hours, and I actually had energy, you know? I'd hang out with Dave, maybe shoot some pool on the weekends, grab a couple beers, just doing my normal routine. I had motivation. I was actually looking forward to getting my own spot, even if it was just a single room in Bridgeport. \n\nBut then... man, it was like a switch flipped, but only because of this place. All this... this exhaustion, this anger, this feeling like I don't even want to go to work because I'm so drained... it all started within the first couple weeks of finding the water damage and realizing the heat was completely busted. \n\nLike I was saying about that first storm, right? That first week or so, before the rain, I was okay. I was setting up my little TV on a crate, putting my work boots by the door, thinking I was getting settled. But then the water started coming in, and the temperature dropped, and suddenly I'm spending all my free time emptying buckets and shivering under a sleeping bag. That's exactly when it hit me. That's when I started losing my drive. Because how are you supposed to stay motivated when you're fighting a losing battle against a ceiling that won't stop leaking? It's a hundred percent the room, Doc. If I wasn't dealing with this landlord and this freezing, wet box, I'd be my regular self. I know I would be. I never used to feel like this, not ever. It's just this situation, it's dragging me down so fast, and it all started right after the leaks began.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your low mood or lack of energy happens almost every day, regardless of what's going on around you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's the thing... it's not like it's just happening out of nowhere, you know? It's not like I'm walking around on a sunny day with a pocket full of cash and suddenly I just feel terrible for no reason. It's not regardless of what's going on, it's *because* of what's going on. 

I mean, yeah, I guess you could say I feel this way pretty much every day right now, but that's only because every single day I have to wake up in that icebox. Like, yesterday, right? I woke up, and I was actually feeling okay for a split second, just for a minute before my brain totally booted up. I was dreaming about something, I don't even remember what, but I was warm. And then I open my eyes, and I can literally see my breath in the air. Inside my own room! And instantly, boom, all the energy just drains right out of me. It's like someone pulled the plug. 

So it's not some random low mood that just follows me around no matter what I'm doing. If I'm at the site, and the sun is out, and me and Dave are moving dirt and joking around, I actually feel alright for a little bit. I forget about it. But then the shift ends. And I start walking to the bus stop, and I realize I have to go back to Bridgeport, back to the buckets and the drafty windows, and that's when it hits me like a ton of bricks. The dread just settles right in my stomach. So yeah, it's every day, because the problem is there every day, but it's completely tied to that room. If you took me out of that room right now and put me in a normal, warm apartment where the ceiling isn't caving in, I swear to you, this whole lack of energy thing would vanish overnight. It's the situation, Doc, it's not just me being down for the sake of being down.

See, I had to move into this new room a little while back—just a cheap place, you know, 'cause rent is crazy and I just needed a spot—but the place is completely busted up. I mean, the landlord swore it was good to go, but nobody told me the ceiling was practically caving in and the radiator is an absolute joke. \n\nSo every single night, instead of getting some actual sleep, I'm up at like two or three in the morning with a bucket and some caulk trying to fix these stupid leaks, or I'm freezing half to death trying to bang on the pipes to get the heat to kick on. And it's just... it's bleeding into everything else, you know? Like, I'm dragging myself into work—I do construction, mostly heavy labor stuff, hauling and whatever—and I'm rolling in late constantly. The foreman is already on my case about it. \n\nAnd because I'm so dead tired and just... wound up from dealing with that stupid room all night, I'm snapping at the other guys on the crew over nothing. Just totally losing my temper if someone even looks at me wrong. Plus, I'm making really dumb mistakes, messing up stuff I normally handle in my sleep. Just dropping things, measuring wrong, you name it. It's like ever since I hauled my stuff into that dump, my whole life is just spinning out, and I can't seem to get my head straight enough to just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest or not getting pleasure from things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like I don't *want* to do stuff. Like, I still love watching the games on Sunday, you know? Usually, I'd grab a six-pack, maybe head over to my buddy Dave's place, or just kick back and watch football. I still *want* to do that. It's not like I don't care about it or anything. It's just... man, how am I supposed to actually enjoy a game when I'm sitting there dead on my feet, thinking about the fact that I gotta go back to that freezing room and probably patch another leak? \n\nOr like, last weekend, I was supposed to help my cousin swap out the alternator on his Chevy—which is something I usually really get a kick out of, you know? I like messing around with engines, it's a good time, totally different from just hauling heavy junk around the site all day. And I totally still wanted to do it! I was looking forward to it all week. I just couldn't, because I spent my whole Saturday morning arguing with the guy at the hardware store about the right kind of sealant for this window frame that's completely rotting out on me. \n\nSo, yeah, I still like all my regular stuff. I still get a laugh out of the guys at work when I'm not biting their heads off. I just... I don't have the time or the energy for any of it right now because every spare second is just sucked up by dealing with this nightmare apartment. But the interest is definitely still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about your housing situation even when you're not directly dealing with it, like when you're at work or trying to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, man, it's... it's like it's glued to the inside of my skull. It's constantly there. Like, even when I'm at the site, right? I'll be hauling a stack of drywall or mixing mud, and normally, I'd just be focused on getting the job done, maybe shooting the breeze with the guys. But now? I'm carrying this heavy stuff, and my brain is just going a mile a minute about that room. I'll be thinking, \"Did I put the bucket in the right spot under that one pipe? What if the drip moved? What if it's soaking into the floorboards right now and the guy below me is gonna start screaming about water damage?\" \n\nAnd it's not just the leaks. It's the cold, too. I'll be sweating on the site because I'm working hard, but I'm dreading the moment I have to clock out because I know I'm going back to a place that feels like a meat locker. I catch myself zoning out while the foreman is giving directions, just trying to mentally calculate if I have enough cash left this week to buy a decent space heater, but then I remember the wiring in that place is so shot that if I plug a heater in, I'll probably blow a fuse for the whole floor. \n\nEven when I try to relax, like if I actually make it to Dave's place for a bit, I can't even focus on the game. He'll be talking about a play, and I'm just sitting there staring at my phone, wondering if I should text the landlord again, even though I know the guy is just gonna ignore me or tell me he'll \"get to it next week\" like he always does. It's just... it's exhausting, you know? It's like I'm never actually away from that stupid room, even when I'm miles away from it. It's just eating up all my headspace, every single day.\n\nDoctor: How do you usually feel when you think about your housing situation—do you feel mostly frustrated, sad, or some combination of both?\n\nPatient: Man, it's... it's mostly just this huge, overwhelming frustration, you know? Like, I wouldn't say I'm sad, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around crying about it or feeling sorry for myself. It's just... I'm so incredibly ticked off all the time. I mean, think about it—I bust my hump all day at the site, hauling rock, pouring concrete, dealing with all the dust and the noise, and for what? Just to hand over my hard-earned cash to some slumlord who can't even be bothered to make sure the roof over my head actually keeps the water out. \n\nIt makes my blood boil just talking about it. Like, I get so angry I can feel my chest getting tight, and my jaw just clenches up. I'll be lying there at 3 AM, freezing, listening to that *drip, drip, drip* into the bucket, and I just want to put my fist through the wall. But I can't even do that, because then I'd lose my deposit, assuming I even get it back in the first place, which I probably won't, knowing this guy. \n\nAnd it's frustrating because I feel so stuck. I don't have the money to just up and move again. You know how much it costs to get a new place? First, last, security deposit—it's thousands of dollars that I just don't have, especially since I'm missing hours at work now because I'm oversleeping from being up all night. So I'm trapped in this freezing, wet box, and there's literally nothing I can do about it except keep patching leaks and arguing with a guy who doesn't care. So yeah, sad isn't the word. It's just this constant, grinding anger and frustration that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your housing situation and dealing with these problems?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been going on for about a month now. Yeah, right around a month. Because, see, I moved into this place in Bridgeport—just a single room, really, not even a real apartment, but it was all I could swing at the time—I moved in right at the beginning of the month. I thought, you know, \"Okay, it's cheap, it's not great, but it's a roof.\" But man, it wasn't even a week before the whole thing just started falling apart on me. \n\nI remember the exact day it started getting really bad, actually. It was that first big rainstorm we had a few weeks back, remember that? It was a Tuesday, because I had just gotten paid on Monday, and I was thinking about maybe grabbing a pizza to celebrate getting settled in. But then the rain hit, and I'm sitting there on my mattress, and suddenly I feel this cold drop hit the back of my neck. I look up, and there's a water stain on the ceiling that's just spreading like crazy. Before I knew it, it wasn't just a drip, it was practically raining inside my room. \n\nSo yeah, ever since then, it's just been this constant battle. It's been about four weeks of me trying to patch things up, trying to get the landlord on the phone, trying to stay warm... and it's just been building and building. Every single day for the last month, it's been the same routine. Wake up freezing, check the buckets, go to work exhausted, come home, check the buckets again, try to get a hold of the landlord, get ignored, go to sleep freezing. It's been a solid month of this nightmare, and I don't see an end to it anytime soon, you know? It feels like it's been a year, honestly, but yeah, on the calendar, it's been about a month since shortly after I moved into that room in Bridgeport.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing motivation even before thinking about your housing situation, or do these feelings only come up when you're dealing with the problems in your room?\n\nPatient: Nah, man, before this? I was... I was completely fine. Like, seriously, I was doing pretty normal before I moved into that room. I mean, sure, work is always tough, hauling all that rock takes it out of you, but I wasn't feeling down or dragging my feet like this. I'd wake up, grab my coffee from the bodega, head to the site, put in my hours, and I actually had energy, you know? I'd hang out with Dave, maybe shoot some pool on the weekends, grab a couple beers, just doing my normal routine. I had motivation. I was actually looking forward to getting my own spot, even if it was just a single room in Bridgeport. \n\nBut then... man, it was like a switch flipped, but only because of this place. All this... this exhaustion, this anger, this feeling like I don't even want to go to work because I'm so drained... it all started within the first couple weeks of finding the water damage and realizing the heat was completely busted. \n\nLike I was saying about that first storm, right? That first week or so, before the rain, I was okay. I was setting up my little TV on a crate, putting my work boots by the door, thinking I was getting settled. That's when I started losing my drive. Because how are you supposed to stay motivated when you're fighting a losing battle against a ceiling that won't stop leaking? It's a hundred percent the room, Doc. If I wasn't dealing with this landlord and this freezing, wet box, I'd be my regular self. I know I would be. I never used to feel like this, not ever. It's just this situation, it's dragging me down so fast, and it all started right after the leaks began.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low mood or lack of energy happens almost every day, regardless of what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing... it's not like it's just happening out of nowhere, you know? Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, the thing is, doc... it’s getting to the point where I don’t even really have moments where it’s not affecting me, because it’s bleeding into literally everything else I do. Honestly, that’s the main reason I even came in here to talk to somebody. Because ever since I moved into that busted-up room, it’s not just about being miserable while I'm sitting in it. It's taking over my whole life. 

I mean, think about it—I’m spending half the night, almost every night, staying up fixing leaks or trying to rig the heat so I don't freeze to death, right? I'm up at 3 AM with a roll of duct tape and an old towel just trying to keep my work boots from getting soaked. So then the alarm goes off a few hours later, and I'm just completely wrecked. I end up dragging myself late to work, which I never used to do. I was always the guy there ten minutes early with a coffee, and now my foreman is looking at his watch every time I walk onto the site. 

And then, because I'm so exhausted and just... wound up tight as a spring, I'm snapping at people. Like, my buddy Dave just asked me a basic question about where I put the pry bar the other day, and I completely bit his head off. I yelled at him over nothing! I felt terrible about it later, but in the moment, I just have zero fuse left. And on top of that, I’m messing up stuff at the site that I normally handle in my sleep. Basic measurements, hauling the right materials, mixing... my brain is just so fried from dealing with this nightmare of an apartment that I can't even do my job right anymore. 

So yeah, even if I'm at work and I'm not actively thinking about the water stain on my ceiling at that exact second, I'm still feeling all that frustration because I'm screwing up my tasks and yelling at my friends. It's like a domino effect, you know? It all traces right back to that room.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hey... so, I guess I'm here because... man, I don't even really know where to start. Everything's just kind of a mess right now. See, I had to move into this new room a little while back—just a cheap place, you know, 'cause rent is crazy and I just needed a spot—but the place is completely busted up. I mean, the landlord swore it was good to go, but nobody told me the ceiling was practically caving in and the radiator is an absolute joke. \n\nSo every single night, instead of getting some actual sleep, I'm up at like two or three in the morning with a bucket and some caulk trying to fix these stupid leaks, or I'm freezing half to death trying to bang on the pipes to get the heat to kick on. And it's just... it's bleeding into everything else, you know? Like, I'm dragging myself into work—I do construction, mostly heavy labor stuff, hauling and whatever—and I'm rolling in late constantly. The foreman is already on my case about it. \n\nAnd because I'm so dead tired and just... wound up from dealing with that stupid room all night, I'm snapping at the other guys on the crew over nothing. Just totally losing my temper if someone even looks at me wrong. Plus, I'm making really dumb mistakes, messing up stuff I normally handle in my sleep. Just dropping things, measuring wrong, you name it. It's like ever since I hauled my stuff into that dump, my whole life is just spinning out, and I can't seem to get my head straight enough to just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest or not getting pleasure from things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like I don't *want* to do stuff. Like, I still love watching the games on Sunday, you know? Usually, I'd grab a six-pack, maybe head over to my buddy Dave's place, or just kick back and watch football. I still *want* to do that. It's not like I don't care about it or anything. It's just... man, how am I supposed to actually enjoy a game when I'm sitting there dead on my feet, thinking about the fact that I gotta go back to that freezing room and probably patch another leak? \n\nOr like, last weekend, I was supposed to help my cousin swap out the alternator on his Chevy—which is something I usually really get a kick out of, you know? I like messing around with engines, it's a good time, totally different from just hauling heavy junk around the site all day. And I totally still wanted to do it! I was looking forward to it all week. I just couldn't, because I spent my whole Saturday morning arguing with the guy at the hardware store about the right kind of sealant for this window frame that's completely rotting out on me. \n\nSo, yeah, I still like all my regular stuff. I still get a laugh out of the guys at work when I'm not biting their heads off. I just... I don't have the time or the energy for any of it right now because every spare second is just sucked up by dealing with this nightmare apartment. But the interest is definitely still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about your housing situation even when you're not directly dealing with it, like when you're at work or trying to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, man, it's... it's like it's glued to the inside of my skull. It's constantly there. Like, even when I'm at the site, right? I'll be hauling a stack of drywall or mixing mud, and normally, I'd just be focused on getting the job done, maybe shooting the breeze with the guys. But now? I'm carrying this heavy stuff, and my brain is just going a mile a minute about that room. I'll be thinking, \"Did I put the bucket in the right spot under that one pipe? What if the drip moved? What if it's soaking into the floorboards right now and the guy below me is gonna start screaming about water damage?\" \n\nAnd it's not just the leaks. It's the cold, too. I'll be sweating on the site because I'm working hard, but I'm dreading the moment I have to clock out because I know I'm going back to a place that feels like a meat locker. I catch myself zoning out while the foreman is giving directions, just trying to mentally calculate if I have enough cash left this week to buy a decent space heater, but then I remember the wiring in that place is so shot that if I plug a heater in, I'll probably blow a fuse for the whole floor. \n\nEven when I try to relax, like if I actually make it to Dave's place for a bit, I can't even focus on the game. He'll be talking about a play, and I'm just sitting there staring at my phone, wondering if I should text the landlord again, even though I know the guy is just gonna ignore me or tell me he'll \"get to it next week\" like he always does. It's just... it's exhausting, you know? It's like I'm never actually away from that stupid room, even when I'm miles away from it. It's just eating up all my headspace, every single day.\n\nDoctor: How do you usually feel when you think about your housing situation—do you feel mostly frustrated, sad, or some combination of both?\n\nPatient: Man, it's... it's mostly just this huge, overwhelming frustration, you know? Like, I wouldn't say I'm sad, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around crying about it or feeling sorry for myself. It's just... I'm so incredibly ticked off all the time. I mean, think about it—I bust my hump all day at the site, hauling rock, pouring concrete, dealing with all the dust and the noise, and for what? Just to hand over my hard-earned cash to some slumlord who can't even be bothered to make sure the roof over my head actually keeps the water out. \n\nIt makes my blood boil just talking about it. Like, I get so angry I can feel my chest getting tight, and my jaw just clenches up. I'll be lying there at 3 AM, freezing, listening to that *drip, drip, drip* into the bucket, and I just want to put my fist through the wall. But I can't even do that, because then I'd lose my deposit, assuming I even get it back in the first place, which I probably won't, knowing this guy. \n\nAnd it's frustrating because I feel so stuck. I don't have the money to just up and move again. You know how much it costs to get a new place? First, last, security deposit—it's thousands of dollars that I just don't have, especially since I'm missing hours at work now because I'm oversleeping from being up all night. So I'm trapped in this freezing, wet box, and there's literally nothing I can do about it except keep patching leaks and arguing with a guy who doesn't care. So yeah, sad isn't the word. It's just this constant, grinding anger and frustration that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your housing situation and dealing with these problems?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been going on for about a month now. Yeah, right around a month. Because, see, I moved into this place in Bridgeport—just a single room, really, not even a real apartment, but it was all I could swing at the time—I moved in right at the beginning of the month. I thought, you know, \"Okay, it's cheap, it's not great, but it's a roof.\" But man, it wasn't even a week before the whole thing just started falling apart on me. \n\nI remember the exact day it started getting really bad, actually. It was that first big rainstorm we had a few weeks back, remember that? It was a Tuesday, because I had just gotten paid on Monday, and I was thinking about maybe grabbing a pizza to celebrate getting settled in. But then the rain hit, and I'm sitting there on my mattress, and suddenly I feel this cold drop hit the back of my neck. I look up, and there's a water stain on the ceiling that's just spreading like crazy. Before I knew it, it wasn't just a drip, it was practically raining inside my room. \n\nSo yeah, ever since then, it's just been this constant battle. It's been about four weeks of me trying to patch things up, trying to get the landlord on the phone, trying to stay warm... and it's just been building and building. Every single day for the last month, it's been the same routine. Wake up freezing, check the buckets, go to work exhausted, come home, check the buckets again, try to get a hold of the landlord, get ignored, go to sleep freezing. It's been a solid month of this nightmare, and I don't see an end to it anytime soon, you know? It feels like it's been a year, honestly, but yeah, on the calendar, it's been about a month since shortly after I moved into that room in Bridgeport.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing motivation even before thinking about your housing situation, or do these feelings only come up when you're dealing with the problems in your room?\n\nPatient: Nah, man, before this? I was... I was completely fine. Like, seriously, I was doing pretty normal before I moved into that room. I mean, sure, work is always tough, hauling all that rock takes it out of you, but I wasn't feeling down or dragging my feet like this. I'd wake up, grab my coffee from the bodega, head to the site, put in my hours, and I actually had energy, you know? I'd hang out with Dave, maybe shoot some pool on the weekends, grab a couple beers, just doing my normal routine. I had motivation. I was actually looking forward to getting my own spot, even if it was just a single room in Bridgeport. \n\nBut then... man, it was like a switch flipped, but only because of this place. All this... this exhaustion, this anger, this feeling like I don't even want to go to work because I'm so drained... it all started within the first couple weeks of finding the water damage and realizing the heat was completely busted. \n\nLike I was saying about that first storm, right? That first week or so, before the rain, I was okay. I was setting up my little TV on a crate, putting my work boots by the door, thinking I was getting settled. But then the water started coming in, and the temperature dropped, and suddenly I'm spending all my free time emptying buckets and shivering under a sleeping bag. That's exactly when it hit me. That's when I started losing my drive. Because how are you supposed to stay motivated when you're fighting a losing battle against a ceiling that won't stop leaking? It's a hundred percent the room, Doc. If I wasn't dealing with this landlord and this freezing, wet box, I'd be my regular self. I know I would be. I never used to feel like this, not ever. It's just this situation, it's dragging me down so fast, and it all started right after the leaks began.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low mood or lack of energy happens almost every day, regardless of what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing... it's not like it's just happening out of nowhere, you know? It's not like I'm walking around on a sunny day with a pocket full of cash and suddenly I just feel terrible for no reason. It's not regardless of what's going on, it's *because* of what's going on. \n\nI mean, yeah, I guess you could say I feel this way pretty much every day right now, but that's only because every single day I have to wake up in that icebox. Like, yesterday, right? I woke up, and I was actually feeling okay for a split second, just for a minute before my brain totally booted up. I was dreaming about something, I don't even remember what, but I was warm. And then I open my eyes, and I can literally see my breath in the air. Inside my own room! And instantly, boom, all the energy just drains right out of me. It's like someone pulled the plug. \n\nSo it's not some random low mood that just follows me around no matter what I'm doing. If I'm at the site, and the sun is out, and me and Dave are moving dirt and joking around, I actually feel alright for a little bit. I forget about it. But then the shift ends. And I start walking to the bus stop, and I realize I have to go back to Bridgeport, back to the buckets and the drafty windows, and that's when it hits me like a ton of bricks. The dread just settles right in my stomach. So yeah, it's every day, because the problem is there every day, but it's completely tied to that room. If you took me out of that room right now and put me in a normal, warm apartment where the ceiling isn't caving in, I swear to you, this whole lack of energy thing would vanish overnight. It's the situation, Doc, it's not just me being down for the sake of being down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these feelings of low mood or frustration happening even in moments when your housing situation wasn’t on your mind, like when you were briefly feeling okay this morning before you remembered the cold?\n\nPatient: Well, the thing is, doc... it’s getting to the point where I don’t even really have moments where it’s not affecting me, because it’s bleeding into literally everything else I do. Honestly, that’s the main reason I even came in here to talk to somebody. Because ever since I moved into that busted-up room, it’s not just about being miserable while I'm sitting in it. It's taking over my whole life. \n\nI mean, think about it—I’m spending half the night, almost every night, staying up fixing leaks or trying to rig the heat so I don't freeze to death, right? I'm up at 3 AM with a roll of duct tape and an old towel just trying to keep my work boots from getting soaked. So then the alarm goes off a few hours later, and I'm just completely wrecked. I end up dragging myself late to work, which I never used to do. I was always the guy there ten minutes early with a coffee, and now my foreman is looking at his watch every time I walk onto the site. \n\nAnd then, because I'm so exhausted and just... wound up tight as a spring, I'm snapping at people. No, I mean... it's not like I'm completely numb or anything. I can still laugh and have a good time if I'm actually doing something fun. Like, just this past Saturday, right? Just totally losing my temper if someone even looks at me wrong. Plus, I'm making really dumb mistakes, messing up stuff I normally handle in my sleep. Just dropping things, measuring wrong, you name it. It's like ever since I hauled my stuff into that dump, my whole life is just spinning out, and I can't seem to get my head straight enough to just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest or not getting pleasure from things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like I don't *want* to do stuff. Like, I still love watching the games on Sunday, you know? I'm carrying this heavy stuff, and my brain is just going a mile a minute about that room. I'll be thinking, \"Did I put the bucket in the right spot under that one pipe? What if the drip moved? What if it's soaking into the floorboards right now and the guy below me is gonna start screaming about water damage?\" \n\nAnd it's not just the leaks. It's the cold, too. I'll be sweating on the site because I'm working hard, but I'm dreading the moment I have to clock out because I know I'm going back to a place that feels like a meat locker. I catch myself zoning out while the foreman is giving directions, just trying to mentally calculate if I have enough cash left this week to buy a decent space heater, but then I remember the wiring in that place is so shot that if I plug a heater in, I'll probably blow a fuse for the whole floor. \n\nEven when I try to relax, like if I actually make it to Dave's place for a bit, I can't even focus on the game. He'll be talking about a play, and I'm just sitting there staring at my phone, wondering if I should text the landlord again, even though I know the guy is just gonna ignore me or tell me he'll \"get to it next week\" like he always does. It's just... it's exhausting, you know? It's like I'm never actually away from that stupid room, even when I'm miles away from it. It's just eating up all my headspace, every single day.\n\nDoctor: How do you usually feel when you think about your housing situation—do you feel mostly frustrated, sad, or some combination of both?\n\nPatient: Man, it's... it's mostly just this huge, overwhelming frustration, you know? I mean, think about it—I bust my hump all day at the site, hauling rock, pouring concrete, dealing with all the dust and the noise, and for what? Just to hand over my hard-earned cash to some slumlord who can't even be bothered to make sure the roof over my head actually keeps the water out. \n\nIt makes my blood boil just talking about it. Like, I get so angry I can feel my chest getting tight, and my jaw just clenches up. I'll be lying there at 3 AM, freezing, listening to that *drip, drip, drip* into the bucket, and I just want to put my fist through the wall. But I can't even do that, because then I'd lose my deposit, assuming I even get it back in the first place, which I probably won't, knowing this guy. \n\nAnd it's frustrating because I feel so stuck. I don't have the money to just up and move again. You know how much it costs to get a new place? First, last, security deposit—it's thousands of dollars that I just don't have, especially since I'm missing hours at work now because I'm oversleeping from being up all night. So I'm trapped in this freezing, wet box, and there's literally nothing I can do about it except keep patching leaks and arguing with a guy who doesn't care. So yeah, sad isn't the word. It's just this constant, grinding anger and frustration that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your housing situation and dealing with these problems?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been going on for about a month now. Yeah, right around a month. Because, see, I moved into this place in Bridgeport—just a single room, really, not even a real apartment, but it was all I could swing at the time—I moved in right at the beginning of the month. I thought, you know, \"Okay, it's cheap, it's not great, but it's a roof.\" But man, it wasn't even a week before the whole thing just started falling apart on me. \n\nI remember the exact day it started getting really bad, actually. It was that first big rainstorm we had a few weeks back, remember that? It was a Tuesday, because I had just gotten paid on Monday, and I was thinking about maybe grabbing a pizza to celebrate getting settled in. But then the rain hit, and I'm sitting there on my mattress, and suddenly I feel this cold drop hit the back of my neck. I look up, and there's a water stain on the ceiling that's just spreading like crazy. Before I knew it, it wasn't just a drip, it was practically raining inside my room. \n\nSo yeah, ever since then, it's just been this constant battle. It's been about four weeks of me trying to patch things up, trying to get the landlord on the phone, trying to stay warm... and it's just been building and building. Every single day for the last month, it's been the same routine. Wake up freezing, check the buckets, go to work exhausted, come home, check the buckets again, try to get a hold of the landlord, get ignored, go to sleep freezing. It's been a solid month of this nightmare, and I don't see an end to it anytime soon, you know? It feels like it's been a year, honestly, but yeah, on the calendar, it's been about a month since shortly after I moved into that room in Bridgeport.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing motivation even before thinking about your housing situation, or do these feelings only come up when you're dealing with the problems in your room?\n\nPatient: Nah, man, before this? I was... I was completely fine. Like, seriously, I was doing pretty normal before I moved into that room. I mean, sure, work is always tough, hauling all that rock takes it out of you, but I wasn't feeling down or dragging my feet like this. I'd wake up, grab my coffee from the bodega, head to the site, put in my hours, and I actually had energy, you know? I'd hang out with Dave, maybe shoot some pool on the weekends, grab a couple beers, just doing my normal routine. I had motivation. I was actually looking forward to getting my own spot, even if it was just a single room in Bridgeport. \n\nBut then... man, it was like a switch flipped, but only because of this place. All this... this exhaustion, this anger, this feeling like I don't even want to go to work because I'm so drained... it all started within the first couple weeks of finding the water damage and realizing the heat was completely busted. \n\nLike I was saying about that first storm, right? That first week or so, before the rain, I was okay. I was setting up my little TV on a crate, putting my work boots by the door, thinking I was getting settled. But then the water started coming in, and the temperature dropped, and suddenly I'm spending all my free time emptying buckets and shivering under a sleeping bag. That's exactly when it hit me. That's when I started losing my drive. Because how are you supposed to stay motivated when you're fighting a losing battle against a ceiling that won't stop leaking? It's a hundred percent the room, Doc. If I wasn't dealing with this landlord and this freezing, wet box, I'd be my regular self. I know I would be. I never used to feel like this, not ever. It's just this situation, it's dragging me down so fast, and it all started right after the leaks began.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low mood or lack of energy happens almost every day, regardless of what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing... it's not like it's just happening out of nowhere, you know? It's not like I'm walking around on a sunny day with a pocket full of cash and suddenly I just feel terrible for no reason. It's not regardless of what's going on, it's *because* of what's going on. \n\nI mean, yeah, I guess you could say I feel this way pretty much every day right now, but that's only because every single day I have to wake up in that icebox. Like, yesterday, right? I woke up, and I was actually feeling okay for a split second, just for a minute before my brain totally booted up. I was dreaming about something, I don't even remember what, but I was warm. And then I open my eyes, and I can literally see my breath in the air. Inside my own room! And instantly, boom, all the energy just drains right out of me. It's like someone pulled the plug. \n\nSo it's not some random low mood that just follows me around no matter what I'm doing. If I'm at the site, and the sun is out, and me and Dave are moving dirt and joking around, I actually feel alright for a little bit. I forget about it. But then the shift ends. And I start walking to the bus stop, and I realize I have to go back to Bridgeport, back to the buckets and the drafty windows, and that's when it hits me like a ton of bricks. The dread just settles right in my stomach. So yeah, it's every day, because the problem is there every day, but it's completely tied to that room. If you took me out of that room right now and put me in a normal, warm apartment where the ceiling isn't caving in, I swear to you, this whole lack of energy thing would vanish overnight. It's the situation, Doc, it's not just me being down for the sake of being down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these feelings of low mood or frustration happening even in moments when your housing situation wasn’t on your mind, like when you were briefly feeling okay this morning before you remembered the cold?\n\nPatient: Well, the thing is, doc... it’s getting to the point where I don’t even really have moments where it’s not affecting me, because it’s bleeding into literally everything else I do. Honestly, that’s the main reason I even came in here to talk to somebody. Because ever since I moved into that busted-up room, it’s not just about being miserable while I'm sitting in it. It's taking over my whole life. \n\nI mean, think about it—I’m spending half the night, almost every night, staying up fixing leaks or trying to rig the heat so I don't freeze to death, right? I'm up at 3 AM with a roll of duct tape and an old towel just trying to keep my work boots from getting soaked. So then the alarm goes off a few hours later, and I'm just completely wrecked. I end up dragging myself late to work, which I never used to do. I was always the guy there ten minutes early with a coffee, and now my foreman is looking at his watch every time I walk onto the site. \n\nAnd then, because I'm so exhausted and just... wound up tight as a spring, I'm snapping at people. Like, my buddy Dave just asked me a basic question about where I put the pry bar the other day, and I completely bit his head off. I yelled at him over nothing! I felt terrible about it later, but in the moment, I just have zero fuse left. And on top of that, I’m messing up stuff at the site that I normally handle in my sleep. Basic measurements, hauling the right materials, mixing... my brain is just so fried from dealing with this nightmare of an apartment that I can't even do my job right anymore. \n\nSo yeah, even if I'm at work and I'm not actively thinking about the water stain on my ceiling at that exact second, I'm still feeling all that frustration because I'm screwing up my tasks and yelling at my friends. It's like a domino effect, you know? It all traces right back to that room.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to feel happy or experience joy, even during moments when things are going well or you're doing something you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I mean... it's not like I'm completely numb or anything. I can still laugh and have a good time if I'm actually doing something fun. Like, just this past Saturday, right? Dave invited me over to his place to watch the fights. He ordered a bunch of wings from that spot down on 4th Street—the one with the crazy garlic parm sauce, you know the place? Anyway, we were just sitting there in his living room, drinking a couple of Coronas, yelling at the TV because the ref made this absolutely garbage call in the third round. \n\nAnd man, for those couple of hours, I was having a blast. I was genuinely happy. I wasn't sitting there brooding or feeling miserable about my life. I was laughing so hard at one point because Dave dropped a wing right on his girlfriend's new rug, and his dog just snatched it up whole before he could even blink. Dave was freaking out trying to get the dog to spit it out because he knew his girl was gonna kill him over the grease stain, and I was just dying laughing. \n\nSo, yeah, I can definitely still feel good. It's not like my brain forgot how to be happy. If I'm hanging out, playing pool, watching a game, I enjoy it just like I always did. I still get a kick out of things. The only problem is that the fun just completely evaporates the second I realize the night's over and I have to catch the bus back to my freezing, leaky room. But during the actual good moments? Nah, I'm fully there. I can still enjoy a cold beer and a good joke, no problem. It's just the hanging cloud of that apartment waiting for me that ruins it afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can still laugh and have a good time if I'm actually doing something fun. Like, just this past Saturday, right? Dave invited me over to his place to watch the fights. He ordered a bunch of wings from that spot down on 4th Street—the one with the crazy garlic parm sauce, you know the place? Anyway, we were just sitting there in his living room, drinking a couple of Coronas, yelling at the TV because the ref made this absolutely garbage call in the third round. \n\nAnd man, for those couple of hours, I was having a blast. It's just the hanging cloud of that apartment waiting for me that ruins it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not dealing with the problems at your apartment, do you ever feel down or lose interest in things for reasons that don’t seem connected to your housing situation?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. We got a huge contract for that new commercial development over on West End, so the hours are there, the overtime is there if I want it, and the pay is steady. We got a huge contract for that new commercial development over on West End, so the hours are there, the overtime is there if I want it, and the pay is steady. Even my truck—which usually gives me grief this time of year—is running great because I just replaced the alternator back in October. \n\nSo, no, there’s nothing else. I’m a pretty simple guy, doc. I go to work, I hang out with my buddies, I watch the game, I drink a beer. I don't sit around overthinking things or getting sad about, I don't know, life in general. I’ve never been that kind of guy. Before I signed the lease on this dump, I was perfectly fine. I was renting a room from my buddy's cousin over in Southside, and it was small, yeah, but it was warm and the roof didn't leak, and I was happy as a clam. I’d wake up, go to the site, come home, play some Xbox, and I felt completely normal. \n\nSo when you ask if I get down for reasons that aren't connected to the apartment... I really can't think of a single thing. It all comes back to that place. Every single bit of this stress, the tiredness, the feeling miserable... it's a hundred percent because of that room. If I didn't have to deal with that, I wouldn't even be sitting in this chair right now, I promise you that. I just don't have anything else to be upset about.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past two weeks when you weren’t thinking about your housing situation and still felt down, hopeless, or uninterested in things?\n\nPatient: I'm trying to think... but no, I really can't. And it was a good day, you know? The sun was actually out for once, it wasn't too freezing, and I was just doing my job. I wasn't feeling down or hopeless at all. I was actually joking around with the pump truck driver—this guy Mike, he's always got these crazy stories about his brother-in-law's boat—and I was completely fine. Totally normal. I wasn't uninterested in anything, I was right there in the moment, having a decent time at work. \n\nBut then... I don't know, it was maybe around 4:30, and the foreman yells that we're wrapping up. I was golden. \n\nSo if you're asking if I just get sad out of nowhere when the apartment isn't on my mind... no, not once in the last two weeks. Not once since I moved in, really. I just don't have that random sadness, you know? It's a hundred percent tied to the living situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days in the past week where you felt down or lost interest in things, even after your housing situation was temporarily resolved or not on your mind?\n\nPatient: Resolved? I’m still right there in that same room, dealing with the exact same garbage. \n\nI mean, the landlord—this guy Gary—he keeps giving me the runaround. I’ll call him on my lunch break, right? And he’ll swear up and down, \"Oh yeah, I'm sending my guy out Thursday to look at the pipes and fix the draft.\" So Thursday rolls around, I bust my tail at the site to get done early, I take the bus back, and I sit there waiting. So I'm still dealing with the repairs that aren't happening, I'm still emptying that stupid bucket under the sink, and I'm still trying to sleep with a cold breeze blowing right across my face. I really can't say, because it hasn't been resolved. Not even for a day. I'm still right in the thick of it, still dealing with the landlord, still living in the mess. So this isn’t something that just kept going long after it was over, you know? It’s not like the room got fixed and I’m just sitting around still feeling miserable for no reason. The problem is still very much happening, every single day. If Gary actually sent someone with a wrench and some weatherstripping and fixed the place, maybe I could tell you how I'd feel afterward. The mess is still my everyday reality.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That's when I started losing my drive. Because how are you supposed to stay motivated when you're fighting a losing battle against a ceiling that won't stop leaking? It's just this situation, it's dragging me down so fast, and it all started right after the leaks began.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low mood or lack of energy happens almost every day, regardless of what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing... it's not like it's just happening out of nowhere, you know? I was dreaming about something, I don't even remember what, but I was warm. And then I open my eyes, and I can literally see my breath in the air. Inside my own room! ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... no, honestly, I don't think so. I really don't. It's like I was saying before, it's all connected, right? I can't think of a single day in the past week where I just woke up and felt like garbage for no reason. 

Like, take this past Sunday. Sunday is usually my one day to just catch my breath, right? I don't have to be at the site, I don't have to listen to the foreman yell about deadlines. I usually try to just chill out, maybe watch some football if I can get a decent signal on my phone, or just sleep in. And this past Sunday, I actually woke up feeling okay for a second. I really did. The sun was coming through the window, and for about five minutes, I wasn't thinking about the lease, or Gary, or the pipes, or any of it. And in those five minutes? I felt fine. I really did. I didn't feel down, I didn't feel hopeless, I wasn't dreading the day. 

But then... I swung my legs out of bed, and my foot landed right in a puddle. Right in a freezing cold puddle of water because that stupid bucket under the sink had overflowed during the night. And man, I'm telling you, the second my foot hit that water, it was like a dark cloud just dropped right over my head. Instantly. The good mood was gone, the feeling okay was gone, and I was just instantly miserable again. I spent the next two hours mopping up dirty water with my only clean towels and cursing Gary's name to high heaven. 

So, no, I haven't had any days where I felt down when I wasn't thinking about the apartment. Because the apartment won't let me *not* think about it, you know? It's always there, waiting to ruin my day. If I'm not thinking about it, I'm fine. But the second it forces its way back into my brain—which is pretty much all the time—that's when everything goes downhill. It's never just out of nowhere. It's always the room.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hey... so, I guess I'm here because... man, I don't even really know where to start. Everything's just kind of a mess right now. See, I had to move into this new room a little while back—just a cheap place, you know, 'cause rent is crazy and I just needed a spot—but the place is completely busted up. I mean, the landlord swore it was good to go, but nobody told me the ceiling was practically caving in and the radiator is an absolute joke. \n\nSo every single night, instead of getting some actual sleep, I'm up at like two or three in the morning with a bucket and some caulk trying to fix these stupid leaks, or I'm freezing half to death trying to bang on the pipes to get the heat to kick on. Like, I'm dragging myself into work—I do construction, mostly heavy labor stuff, hauling and whatever—and I'm rolling in late constantly. The foreman is already on my case about it. \n\nAnd because I'm so dead tired and just... wound up from dealing with that stupid room all night, I'm snapping at the other guys on the crew over nothing. Just totally losing my temper if someone even looks at me wrong. Plus, I'm making really dumb mistakes, messing up stuff I normally handle in my sleep. Just dropping things, measuring wrong, you name it. It's like ever since I hauled my stuff into that dump, my whole life is just spinning out, and I can't seem to get my head straight enough to just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest or not getting pleasure from things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like I don't *want* to do stuff. Like, I still love watching the games on Sunday, you know? Usually, I'd grab a six-pack, maybe head over to my buddy Dave's place, or just kick back and watch football. I still *want* to do that. It's not like I don't care about it or anything. It's just... man, how am I supposed to actually enjoy a game when I'm sitting there dead on my feet, thinking about the fact that I gotta go back to that freezing room and probably patch another leak? \n\nOr like, last weekend, I was supposed to help my cousin swap out the alternator on his Chevy—which is something I usually really get a kick out of, you know? I like messing around with engines, it's a good time, totally different from just hauling heavy junk around the site all day. And I totally still wanted to do it! I was looking forward to it all week. I just couldn't, because I spent my whole Saturday morning arguing with the guy at the hardware store about the right kind of sealant for this window frame that's completely rotting out on me. \n\nSo, yeah, I still like all my regular stuff. I still get a laugh out of the guys at work when I'm not biting their heads off. I just... I don't have the time or the energy for any of it right now because every spare second is just sucked up by dealing with this nightmare apartment. But the interest is definitely still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about your housing situation even when you're not directly dealing with it, like when you're at work or trying to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, man, it's... it's like it's glued to the inside of my skull. It's constantly there. Like, even when I'm at the site, right? I'll be hauling a stack of drywall or mixing mud, and normally, I'd just be focused on getting the job done, maybe shooting the breeze with the guys. But now? I'm carrying this heavy stuff, and my brain is just going a mile a minute about that room. I'll be thinking, \"Did I put the bucket in the right spot under that one pipe? What if the drip moved? What if it's soaking into the floorboards right now and the guy below me is gonna start screaming about water damage?\" \n\nAnd it's not just the leaks. It's the cold, too. I'll be sweating on the site because I'm working hard, but I'm dreading the moment I have to clock out because I know I'm going back to a place that feels like a meat locker.
