It's just this constant, grinding anger and frustration that I can't shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your housing situation and dealing with these problems?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been going on for about a month now. Yeah, right around a month. Because, see, I moved into this place in Bridgeport—just a single room, really, not even a real apartment, but it was all I could swing at the time—I moved in right at the beginning of the month. I thought, you know, \"Okay, it's cheap, it's not great, but it's a roof.\" But man, it wasn't even a week before the whole thing just started falling apart on me. \n\nI remember the exact day it started getting really bad, actually. It was that first big rainstorm we had a few weeks back, remember that? It was a Tuesday, because I had just gotten paid on Monday, and I was thinking about maybe grabbing a pizza to celebrate getting settled in. But then the rain hit, and I'm sitting there on my mattress, and suddenly I feel this cold drop hit the back of my neck. I look up, and there's a water stain on the ceiling that's just spreading like crazy. Before I knew it, it wasn't just a drip, it was practically raining inside my room. \n\nSo yeah, ever since then, it's just been this constant battle. It's been about four weeks of me trying to patch things up, trying to get the landlord on the phone, trying to stay warm... and it's just been building and building. Every single day for the last month, it's been the same routine. Wake up freezing, check the buckets, go to work exhausted, come home, check the buckets again, try to get a hold of the landlord, get ignored, go to sleep freezing. It's been a solid month of this nightmare, and I don't see an end to it anytime soon, you know? It feels like it's been a year, honestly, but yeah, on the calendar, it's been about a month since shortly after I moved into that room in Bridgeport.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing motivation even before thinking about your housing situation, or do these feelings only come up when you're dealing with the problems in your room?\n\nPatient: Nah, man, before this? I was... I was completely fine. Like, seriously, I was doing pretty normal before I moved into that room. I mean, sure, work is always tough, hauling all that rock takes it out of you, but I wasn't feeling down or dragging my feet like this. I'd wake up, grab my coffee from the bodega, head to the site, put in my hours, and I actually had energy, you know? I'd hang out with Dave, maybe shoot some pool on the weekends, grab a couple beers, just doing my normal routine. I had motivation. I was actually looking forward to getting my own spot, even if it was just a single room in Bridgeport. \n\nBut then... man, it was like a switch flipped, but only because of this place. All this... this exhaustion, this anger, this feeling like I don't even want to go to work because I'm so drained... it all started within the first couple weeks of finding the water damage and realizing the heat was completely busted. \n\nLike I was saying about that first storm, right? That first week or so, before the rain, I was okay. I was setting up my little TV on a crate, putting my work boots by the door, thinking I was getting settled. But then the water started coming in, and the temperature dropped, and suddenly I'm spending all my free time emptying buckets and shivering under a sleeping bag. That's exactly when it hit me. That's when I started losing my drive. Because how are you supposed to stay motivated when you're fighting a losing battle against a ceiling that won't stop leaking? It's a hundred percent the room, Doc. If I wasn't dealing with this landlord and this freezing, wet box, I'd be my regular self. I know I would be. I never used to feel like this, not ever. It's just this situation, it's dragging me down so fast, and it all started right after the leaks began.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low mood or lack of energy happens almost every day, regardless of what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing... it's not like it's just happening out of nowhere, you know? It's not like I'm walking around on a sunny day with a pocket full of cash and suddenly I just feel terrible for no reason. It's not regardless of what's going on, it's *because* of what's going on. \n\nI mean, yeah, I guess you could say I feel this way pretty much every day right now, but that's only because every single day I have to wake up in that icebox. Like, yesterday, right? I woke up, and I was actually feeling okay for a split second, just for a minute before my brain totally booted up. I was dreaming about something, I don't even remember what, but I was warm. And then I open my eyes, and I can literally see my breath in the air. Inside my own room! And instantly, boom, all the energy just drains right out of me. It's like someone pulled the plug. \n\nSo it's not some random low mood that just follows me around no matter what I'm doing. If I'm at the site, and the sun is out, and me and Dave are moving dirt and joking around, I actually feel alright for a little bit. I forget about it. But then the shift ends. And I start walking to the bus stop, and I realize I have to go back to Bridgeport, back to the buckets and the drafty windows, and that's when it hits me like a ton of bricks. The dread just settles right in my stomach. So yeah, it's every day, because the problem is there every day, but it's completely tied to that room. If you took me out of that room right now and put me in a normal, warm apartment where the ceiling isn't caving in, I swear to you, this whole lack of energy thing would vanish overnight. It's the situation, Doc, it's not just me being down for the sake of being down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these feelings of low mood or frustration happening even in moments when your housing situation wasn’t on your mind, like when you were briefly feeling okay this morning before you remembered the cold?\n\nPatient: Well, the thing is, doc... it’s getting to the point where I don’t even really have moments where it’s not affecting me, because it’s bleeding into literally everything else I do. Honestly, that’s the main reason I even came in here to talk to somebody. Because ever since I moved into that busted-up room, it’s not just about being miserable while I'm sitting in it. It's taking over my whole life. \n\nI mean, think about it—I’m spending half the night, almost every night, staying up fixing leaks or trying to rig the heat so I don't freeze to death, right? I'm up at 3 AM with a roll of duct tape and an old towel just trying to keep my work boots from getting soaked. So then the alarm goes off a few hours later, and I'm just completely wrecked. I end up dragging myself late to work, which I never used to do. I was always the guy there ten minutes early with a coffee, and now my foreman is looking at his watch every time I walk onto the site. \n\nAnd then, because I'm so exhausted and just... wound up tight as a spring, I'm snapping at people. Like, my buddy Dave just asked me a basic question about where I put the pry bar the other day, and I completely bit his head off. I yelled at him over nothing! I felt terrible about it later, but in the moment, I just have zero fuse left. And on top of that, I’m messing up stuff at the site that I normally handle in my sleep. Basic measurements, hauling the right materials, mixing... my brain is just so fried from dealing with this nightmare of an apartment that I can't even do my job right anymore. \n\nSo yeah, even if I'm at work and I'm not actively thinking about the water stain on my ceiling at that exact second, I'm still feeling all that frustration because I'm screwing up my tasks and yelling at my friends. It's like a domino effect, you know? It all traces right back to that room.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it difficult to feel happy or experience joy, even during moments when things are going well or you're doing something you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I mean... it's not like I'm completely numb or anything. I can still laugh and have a good time if I'm actually doing something fun. Like, just this past Saturday, right? Dave invited me over to his place to watch the fights. He ordered a bunch of wings from that spot down on 4th Street—the one with the crazy garlic parm sauce, you know the place? Anyway, we were just sitting there in his living room, drinking a couple of Coronas, yelling at the TV because the ref made this absolutely garbage call in the third round. \n\nAnd man, for those couple of hours, I was having a blast. I was genuinely happy. I wasn't sitting there brooding or feeling miserable about my life. I was laughing so hard at one point because Dave dropped a wing right on his girlfriend's new rug, and his dog just snatched it up whole before he could even blink. Dave was freaking out trying to get the dog to spit it out because he knew his girl was gonna kill him over the grease stain, and I was just dying laughing. \n\nSo, yeah, I can definitely still feel good. It's not like my brain forgot how to be happy. If I'm hanging out, playing pool, watching a game, I enjoy it just like I always did. I still get a kick out of things. The only problem is that the fun just completely evaporates the second I realize the night's over and I have to catch the bus back to my freezing, leaky room. But during the actual good moments? Nah, I'm fully there. I can still enjoy a cold beer and a good joke, no problem. It's just the hanging cloud of that apartment waiting for me that ruins it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not dealing with the problems at your apartment, do you ever feel down or lose interest in things for reasons that don’t seem connected to your housing situation?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. That’s what I’m trying to say, you know? It’s literally just the apartment. I don't have anything else going on that would make me feel like this. Like, if you look at the rest of my life, things are actually... well, they're fine. I mean, I don't have any crazy drama with my family or anything. My mom calls me on Sundays, we talk for a bit, everything's good there. I don't have a girlfriend right now, so there's no relationship stress or arguing or any of that kind of headache. And work, I mean, yeah, I told you I’ve been messing up a bit lately because I'm so exhausted, but the job itself? The job is solid. We got a huge contract for that new commercial development over on West End, so the hours are there, the overtime is there if I want it, and the pay is steady. Even my truck—which usually gives me grief this time of year—is running great because I just replaced the alternator back in October. \n\nSo, no, there’s nothing else. I’m a pretty simple guy, doc. I go to work, I hang out with my buddies, I watch the game, I drink a beer. I don't sit around overthinking things or getting sad about, I don't know, life in general. I’ve never been that kind of guy. Before I signed the lease on this dump, I was perfectly fine. I was renting a room from my buddy's cousin over in Southside, and it was small, yeah, but it was warm and the roof didn't leak, and I was happy as a clam. I’d wake up, go to the site, come home, play some Xbox, and I felt completely normal. \n\nSo when you ask if I get down for reasons that aren't connected to the apartment... I really can't think of a single thing. It all comes back to that place. Every single bit of this stress, the tiredness, the feeling miserable... it's a hundred percent because of that room. If I didn't have to deal with that, I wouldn't even be sitting in this chair right now, I promise you that. I just don't have anything else to be upset about.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past two weeks when you weren’t thinking about your housing situation and still felt down, hopeless, or uninterested in things?\n\nPatient: I'm trying to think... but no, I really can't. I mean, it's exactly like I was saying before, it always comes back to the room. \n\nLike, if I look at just this past Wednesday, right? We were pouring concrete for the foundation at the new site. And it was a good day, you know? The sun was actually out for once, it wasn't too freezing, and I was just doing my job. I wasn't feeling down or hopeless at all. I was actually joking around with the pump truck driver—this guy Mike, he's always got these crazy stories about his brother-in-law's boat—and I was completely fine. Totally normal. I wasn't uninterested in anything, I was right there in the moment, having a decent time at work. \n\nBut then... I don't know, it was maybe around 4:30, and the foreman yells that we're wrapping up. And the second—literally the exact second—I started packing up my tools and throwing my hard hat in the truck, it just hit me. This heavy, miserable feeling, just dreading having to take the bus back to that icebox. That's when the hopelessness kicks in. But before that thought crossed my mind? I was golden. \n\nSo if you're asking if I just get sad out of nowhere when the apartment isn't on my mind... no, not once in the last two weeks. Not once since I moved in, really. If my mood is in the gutter, you can bet your last dollar it's because I just remembered the draft coming through my window or that stupid bucket I have to empty under the sink. I just don't have that random sadness, you know? It's a hundred percent tied to the living situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days in the past week where you felt down or lost interest in things, even after your housing situation was temporarily resolved or not on your mind?\n\nPatient: Resolved? Man, I... I wish I could tell you it was resolved, or even just put on pause for a minute, but no. The housing mess hasn’t ended at all. I’m still right there in that same room, dealing with the exact same garbage. \n\nI mean, the landlord—this guy Gary—he keeps giving me the runaround. I’ll call him on my lunch break, right? And he’ll swear up and down, \"Oh yeah, I'm sending my guy out Thursday to look at the pipes and fix the draft.\" So Thursday rolls around, I bust my tail at the site to get done early, I take the bus back, and I sit there waiting. And nobody shows up. Not a knock, not a phone call, nothing. So I'm still dealing with the repairs that aren't happening, I'm still emptying that stupid bucket under the sink, and I'm still trying to sleep with a cold breeze blowing right across my face. It’s like a full-time job just trying to get this guy to do the bare minimum of what I pay rent for. \n\nSo when you ask if I've felt down after the situation was resolved... I really can't say, because it hasn't been resolved. Not even for a day. I'm still right in the thick of it, still dealing with the landlord, still living in the mess. So this isn’t something that just kept going long after it was over, you know? It’s not like the room got fixed and I’m just sitting around still feeling miserable for no reason. The problem is still very much happening, every single day. If Gary actually sent someone with a wrench and some weatherstripping and fixed the place, maybe I could tell you how I'd feel afterward. But right now? The mess is still my everyday reality.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your low mood or frustration only happens when you’re thinking about or dealing with problems related to your apartment, and not at other times?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That’s exactly it. I mean, that’s literally the whole reason I even came in to see you in the first place. Because it’s all tied to that place and what it's making me do. \n\nEver since I signed that lease and moved into that busted-up room, my whole life has just been hijacked by it. It’s not just that I think about it and get mad, it’s that I’m physically dealing with it constantly, and it's ruining everything else. Like, I’m staying up half the night trying to tape up the window frames to keep the heat in, or I’m under the sink with a wrench and a flashlight at 2 AM trying to fix these leaks that Gary refuses to deal with. I'm playing plumber and handyman when I should be sleeping. \n\nAnd because I'm up all night messing with that garbage, I'm waking up completely exhausted. Then I’m dragging myself in late to work, which makes the foreman ride my case the second I step on the site. And because I'm so tired and stressed about the room, I’m just... I'm snapping at people. Like, the other day Mike asked me a simple question about the cement mixer and I just bit his head off for no reason. That’s not me, doc. I don’t do that. I'm usually the easygoing guy. \n\nAnd on top of the snapping, I’m messing up stuff at the site that I normally handle in my sleep. Like, basic measurements or framing that I've done a thousand times, I’m just botching them because my brain is fried from dealing with the apartment all night. So yeah, the frustration, the bad mood, the snapping at my crew—all of it only happens because of what I’m dealing with at that apartment. If I didn't have to spend my whole night fighting with a leaky pipe or freezing my tail off, I wouldn't be screwing up my job and yelling at my buddies, you know? It all traces right back to dealing with that room.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past week where you felt down or lost interest in things, even when you weren’t thinking about your apartment or the problems with it?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, honestly, I don't think so. I really don't. It's like I was saying before, it's all connected, right? I can't think of a single day in the past week where I just woke up and felt like garbage for no reason. \n\nLike, take this past Sunday. Sunday is usually my one day to just catch my breath, right? I don't have to be at the site, I don't have to listen to the foreman yell about deadlines. I usually try to just chill out, maybe watch some football if I can get a decent signal on my phone, or just sleep in. And this past Sunday, I actually woke up feeling okay for a second. I really did. The sun was coming through the window, and for about five minutes, I wasn't thinking about the lease, or Gary, or the pipes, or any of it. And in those five minutes? I felt fine. I really did. I didn't feel down, I didn't feel hopeless, I wasn't dreading the day. \n\nBut then... I swung my legs out of bed, and my foot landed right in a puddle. Right in a freezing cold puddle of water because that stupid bucket under the sink had overflowed during the night. And man, I'm telling you, the second my foot hit that water, it was like a dark cloud just dropped right over my head. Instantly. The good mood was gone, the feeling okay was gone, and I was just instantly miserable again. I spent the next two hours mopping up dirty water with my only clean towels and cursing Gary's name to high heaven. \n\nSo, no, I haven't had any days where I felt down when I wasn't thinking about the apartment. Because the apartment won't let me *not* think about it, you know? It's always there, waiting to ruin my day. If I'm not thinking about it, I'm fine. But the second it forces its way back into my brain—which is pretty much all the time—that's when everything goes downhill. It's never just out of nowhere. It's always the room."
